Ryan Gosling is Officially Reviving The Flintstones for New Project

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Ryan Gosling has been incredibly booked and busy of late. He starred in the lead role in the sci-fi smash hit Project Hail Mary earlier this year. In 2027, he'll travel to the galaxy far, far away in Star Wars: Starfighter. Then in 2028 he'll make his long-awaited MCU debut as Ghost Rider, re-teaming with Starfighter director Shawn Levy. If all that wasn't enough, Gosling has lined up one more unexpected project: The Flintstones.

Gosling's production company, Open Invite, and Warner Bros. are in early development on a live-action Flintstones reboot centered on a grown-up Bamm-Bamm, according to Deadline. Currently, Gosling and his Open Invite partner Jessie Henderson are looking for a writer to take on the project. Later this year Open Invite will premiere the romantic drama Love of Your Life at the Toronto International Film Festival. The movie stars Margaret Qualley (The Dog Stars), Aaron Pierre (Lanterns), and Patrick Schwarzenegger (The White Lotus), among others.

Bamm-Bamm first appeared as a baby in the original 1960s Hanna-Barbera cartoon, as the young son of Barney and Betty Rubble. He was depicted as a teenager in The Pebbles and Bamm-Bamm Show in the 1970s, when he started a band with his sister Pebbles. Bamm-Bamm also appeared in the 1994 live-action Flintstones movie.

As Gosling's project is currently in early development, there is no news about who may play Bamm-Bamm or what the story will be. The big question is whether Gosling will be involved in the project beyond his role as a producer. After his widely acclaimed performance as Ken in Greta Gerwig's Barbie, it seems like the actor would be a perfect fit for Bamm-Bamm. Of course, with his schedule already so busy, he may not want to take on yet another project.

There is also the possibility of a Barbie sequel. Warner Bros. would likely prioritize Gosling's return to Barbie over the Flintstones project, since the former is the studio's highest-grossing film.

Here's What to Gift the Maximalist Friend Who Already Has Everything

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

A fellow maximalist shares niche beauty, fashion, fragrance, and accessory finds your most extra friend may not know about yet.

Do you have a friend who literally has it all? While shopping for a minimalist has its own struggles, you don't know agony unless you're looking for a gift for the maximalist in your life whose apartment is simply filled with stuff. And I know because I'm that friend (and so is every shopping writer in existence).

Knowing your friend well enough to know exactly what to gift them is good advice in general, but it's especially good advice for the maximalist. You have to know what they already have; that's all on you. As a fellow maximalist, what I can help you with in your gift-shopping journey is to give you recommendations on products that they probably want, but won't buy themselves. We're talking niche, colorful, and unique products across categories that we simply know they'll love and want.

Whether it's a beauty oracle deck your witchy friend who collects tarot decks won't have, or indie fashion and skincare brands they might not know exist (but will definitely be obsessed over), keep scrolling to shop my picks for the best gifts for that maximalist friend on your list.

As a maximalist, your friend would want to change their nails every one or two weeks. So why not gift them press-on nails with detailed designs that look like they just got out of a nail appointment? These nails are some of the best (and longest) you can get, with so many design options available.

In collaboration with Heaven Mayhem, OUAI just launched an amulet perfume necklace with one of their signature scents. It features a striking black and silver pendant, a dramatic black tassel, and notes of cocoa, tonka bean, vanilla and rum.

If your maximalist bestie loves fashion, designers, a good documentary, and a chic coffee table book, Marc by Sofia is a perfect gift. This companion book to Sofia Coppola's new documentary offers a look inside Marc Jacob's life and career through conversations between them and personal photos.

Know for a fact that your maximalist friend doesn't have a red light mask? While there are so many masks to choose from, Skin Gym's LED face mask stands out for its 10-minute treatment time and options of red (630nm), blue (465nm), and near-infrared (830nm) lights to help reduce the appearance of fine lines, smooth blemishes, and more.

Your maximalist friend definitely loses their gloves every single winter. I know it because I am her. So, get them these adorable (and cozy) knit gloves from Kurt Geiger, which feature a fuchsia ribbed knit, a rainbow Paddington embroidered badge, and a brown branded leather tag patch.

If you're looking to gift an actual sustainable and chic bag that'll last for years and isn't made of plastic, this cactus leather tote bag from Santos by Monica—with its delicious red colorway, unique geometric shape, and magnetic closure—is the only right choice (in my biased opinion).

Clinique's viral iconic shade Black Honey just got a bedazzled makeover for the holidays. The limited-edition, crystal-coated case will stop anyone in their tracks while you're reapplying.

If your maximalist friend is on the witchy side, they might have a giant collection of tarot and oracle decks. And yet, they might not have this first-of-its-kind (and unique) beauty oracle deck created by an aesthetician and Human Design expert that's perfect for every intuitive beauty lover.

Every maximalist needs at least one extra phone case—whether they just want to change the design any given day or they lost their other one. You can't go wrong with Wildflower cases, and this pop-star-approved one features a bold black star set against a sparkling crystal rhinestone backdrop.

Weird girl approved! Snif always delivers quirky and unique scents, and this pink bottle is no exception. The Lychee Lacquer is a surprisingly bright, fruity, and floral smell, with notes of lychee, nail polish accord, rose, jasmine absolute, vanilla, and musk.

Is there such a thing as too many accessories? This vintage-inspired mulberry silk bandana scarf features a trendy olive green shade, playful polka dot details, hand-rolled edges, and hand-stitched finishing.

If you're worried about gifting skincare to your maximalist friend who has it all, we recommend opting for indie and new brands. Lazy Skincare has only been around for a year, and it already has devoted fans (myself included). Their hero product is Double Duty, a serum-moisturizer duo that's formulated with skin-firming red carmellia extract, smoothing bakuchiol, hydrating silver ear mushroom, and more.

While you could just stumble into a vintage shop and get anything from there for your maximalist friend, why not also get them a new style alongside it? Damson Madder is that brand for anyone who loves their clothes to have an interesting shape while still being wearable—and this trench coat is simply a perfect example of that.

For your maximalist friend who loves a good board game, mahjong might be the next hobby they'll get into (if they aren't into it already). This high-quality tile set is one-of-a-kind, with its intricate designs, vibrant colors, floral motifs, and chic tortoise back.

Eric Bauza’s Final ‘Looney Tunes’ Performance Is ‘Daffy Season,’ Now in Theaters Ahead of ‘The Cat in the Hat’

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

The death of Eric Bauza in October shocked the entertainment industry. An impressively prolific voice actor, it would’ve been nearly impossible for anybody engaging with animation over the past 15 years to never come across his work. Bauza’s death came just two months after the delayed release of “Coyote vs. Acme,” the “Looney Tunes” feature once deemed unreleasable by Warner Bros. Discovery and later sold off to Ketchup Entertainment. The film, which grossed $101 million worldwide, is a true showcase for Bauza, who lends his voice to a plethora of iconic characters, including Bugs Bunny, Daffy Duck, Elmer Fudd, Foghorn Leghorn, Tweety, and Porky Pig. Related Stories Box Office: ‘Godzilla Minus Zero’ Roars with $6.7 Million in Previews, ‘Ramayana: Rise of a Legend’ Earns $3.5 Million David Ellison’s Big Bet: Why the Mogul Thinks Skydance Film Chiefs Dana Goldberg and Josh Greenstein Can Deliver 30 Movies a Year

Bauza became something of the defining voice of modern “Looney Tunes” in his final years. Fans can see him give one final performance with the property in “Daffy Season,” a 7-minute animated short that plays before screenings of “The Cat in the Hat,” opening Friday from Skydance’s Warner Bros. Pictures. A representative for the studio confirmed to IndieWire that the short is Bauza’s final “Looney Tunes” movie. Bauza is also credited as providing additional voices for “The Cat in the Hat.”

“Daffy Season” centers on Daffy Duck, voiced by Bauza. He’s joined by Jeff Bergman as Bugs Bunny, Gabriel Iglesias as Speedy Gonzales, and Jeannie Tirado as Lola Bunny. The plot follows Daffy, turning his beak up at his fellow Tunes as they mindlessly obsess over the game of soccer. Todd Wilderman and Hamish Grieve are co-directors. “Daffy Season” is the first theatrically released “Looney Tunes” short since “Daffy’s Rhapsody” played before “Journey 2: The Mysterious Island” back in 2012.

Before “Daffy Season,” Bauza had already provided some voice work for a “Tunes” musical feature titled “Bye Bye Bunny,” which was canceled after some years of development and production. Other “Looney Tunes” credits from Bauza include the HBO Max series “Looney Tunes Cartoons” and the features “The Day the Earth Blew Up: A Looney Tunes Movie” and “Space Jam: A New Legacy.”

Bauza died at his home in Los Angeles on October 25 as a result of hypertensive cardiovascular disease. In a tribute in The Hollywood Reporter, Warner Bros. Pictures Animation producer Susan Rogers credited Bauza with playing a huge role in reviving the “Looney Tunes.”

“There wasn’t a single short he couldn’t quote or couldn’t reference,” she said. “I don’t think you replace Eric Bauza … The hole he leaves is huge. There are many talented people out there, and the franchise will move on. But there’s only one Eric Bauza.”

Bonnie Wright has been dealing with a difficult situation.

The Harry Potter alum shared that her and husband Andrew Lococo’s son Aster, who was born on Sept. 28, has been making repeated hospital visits due to an undisclosed health issue.

“Our sweet little Aster has been to the hospital 6 times in his 5 weeks of life,” Wright posted to Instagram Nov. 6 alongside a photo of herself holding the newborn, whose face she covered with a white heart emoji. “Getting treatment for a condition we knew he’d be born with. While the 35-year-old did not share details about Aster’s condition, she did thank friends and family for their help in caring for his 3-year-old brother Elio during this challenging time.

"Feeling so lucky to have such supportive family and friends,” Wright added. “Helping take Elio to preschool, cooking us meals, checking in on us. The village is possible, text/call your pals.”

Shortly before Aster’s birth, Wright reflected on her final months of pregnancy, especially how different the experience had been compared to her first child.

“This pregnancy has been physically harder,” she wrote in a July post on her Substack. “I’m chasing a toddler, I’m three years older, I’m running on considerably less sleep than I’d like to admit.”

And becoming a mother also gave the former child star a new perspective on her life, admitting she used to believe that she had to land “somewhere definitive” in order to feel like she was gaining clarity.

“Motherhood has slowly, persistently taught me that nothing is actually that finite,” she confessed. “We are always in progress. Sometimes things move forward and sometimes it genuinely feels like a billion steps back and both are just part of it.”

The Saints, like all teams, pay attention to the things said by their upcoming opponents.

Earlier this week, Browns quarterback Shedeur Sanders seemed to make some less-than-flattering comments about the New Orleans defense. The New Orleans defense heard it, loud and clear.

“We’re gonna come in with a chip on our shoulder, for sure,” Saints defensive end Carl Granderson said Friday. “You know, they been chirping a little bit. We heard what the quarterback . . . said. So, you know, we’re [going to] come in hot and ready to play. . . . It’s gonna be a good game, for sure. We’re gonna come out with the [win].”

Asked Wednesday by reporters whether he has had a chance to study the Saints defense and to explain what he has seen, Sanders flashed a broad smile.

“Yeah, I’ve seen a lot,” Sanders said. “When I smile, just know that means something. I’m maturing. I’m maturing. Like, I could have given a decent answer, but now I’m just going to smile whenever it’s just, like, some questions.”

The better approach for Sanders would have been to project a poker face, and to give an answer that did nothing to attract the attention of the Saints’ defense.

So while he says he’s maturing, the ultimate level of maturity would consist of providing the most vanilla answer possible, praising the Saints and explaining that it will be a major challenge to beat them.

Instead, a Browns player has provided the next opponent — for the second straight week — with a little extra incentive.

Akrotiri on Santorini was buried in ash by the Thera eruption in the 17th or 16th century B.C.E., preserving a remarkably intact Bronze Age settlement. Archaeologists have found multistory buildings, frescoes, pottery, and evidence of maritime trade that offer a rare look at daily life in the Cyclades.

Rumors have been swirling for almost a year now that the next generation of Sony hardware will include both a standard PS6 home console and some sort of handheld device, but according to someone with a whole lot of knowledge and insight into PlayStation hardware launches, that's a recipe for failure.

Shawn Layden, former chairman of SIE Worldwide Studios, recently appeared on the Trophy Room: A PlayStation Show podcast, and he cautioned his former employer against releasing both a handheld and home console at the same time. Having been with PlayStation through the launch of the PSP and PS Vita, Layden says "it was difficult to support both a console and a handheld at the same time," adding, "you almost have to create two companies."

"While they are complimentary, they can also be intention in where you're going, what you're trying to say. And the console, which is the bigger dollar, the bigger audience, has more weight in the marketing room. And you see PSP marketing dollars just like go away," Layden says.

He then goes into a lengthy anecdote (timestamp) about being admonished by Sony's CFO for failing to sell enough PS3s even though PSP sales were "off the chart," but Layden eventually returns to his central point: Sony can be successful in the next console generation, but only if it can focus on supporting just one platform at a time, rather than trying to juggle two distinct machines, i.e. PSP and PS3.

"If Sony only does a handheld next, then I think they have a good chance of winning," says Layden. "But if they're going to launch a handheld and a console at the same time, the company just doesn't have - I don't think any company has the ability to be successful with both."

This isn't the first time Layden has tried to sway PlayStation from launching a home console and handheld in the same generation, having previously attributed the problem to "development resources" as well as "marketing resources."

PS6 development is "progressing as scheduled," Sony assures – even if PlayStation is battling against the RAM shortage and balancing "memory procurement"

Joanna Gaines knows what every fixer upper needs, and (alongside her husband Chip) has proven this episode after episode. They’re specifically credited with popularizing "farmhouse chic" in the late 2010s, which I have to admit is always a favorite of mine. However, there’s one aspect of this trend that doesn’t seem to have fallen out of favor… and honestly, I bet you can guess the one Fixer Upper thing fans bring up to Gaines every single day.

I’m not talking about clawfoot tubs or barn doors... or even the newest Fixer Upper project. I'm talking about a certain fever that continues to run hot. When Gaines stopped by Kylie Kelce’s podcast Not Gonna Lie for a quick chat about her new mansion project, the host quickly brought up the aesthetic that Gaines put on the map. (Have you guessed it yet?) That’s right. We're here to talk about shiplap. Check out the exchange below:

In case you somehow slept through an episode where she used shiplap, I’ll explain it real quick. Shiplap was originally a functional exterior siding technique (primarily for things like ships, coastal homes, or barns) before it became a popular interior design element used during the height of the farmhouse era. Although has it really subsided? Both women were quick to defend the technique, with Kelce even describing it feeling like “a warm hug.” My farm upbringing can see her point as there is something homey about the way shiplap looks.

While shiplap was already around before Fixer Upper, the use of it was definitely popularized by the mom of five. Although when they found it she admittedly didn’t know what it was called. She even referred to the siding as "boards." Still, I'm not going to hold that against her. Fast forward to today, and Gaines still can’t escape it. Once it’s a hashtag, there’s no going back. For this, she blames it on one specific factor:

I’m gonna defend myself here because every now and then I’ll see like on social, it’ll be like ‘the modern farmhouse aesthetic is over.’ Like you hear all this but it’s like in 2013 and 2014 when the modern farmhouse aesthetic was rising. It really was like people wanted the open floorplan. The hardest thing was I would do client meetings at the farm and there was shiplap on the wall so the client would say, ‘I want what your house looks like. Can I have shiplap on the walls?’ Like, everyone got shiplap.

Not sure if this counts as "one of the biggest mistakes" people make with house design, but there is an irony in Gaines’ own aesthetic rubbing off on clients. We’ve continued to see trendy design ideas since she and Chip launched the Magnolia Network, including a livable garden, and I look forward to seeing what they do with the mansion.

For those who don’t know, the couple is facing a new project in Fixer Upper: Chip Bought A Mansion. The title is a bit of a giveaway, but Chip bought an European-inspired mansion overlooking Lake Waco in Texas. Having bought it three years ago, Gaines is now stepping with her signature eye. The series lands on the 2026 TV schedule on December 27 and is expected to have six episodes. It can be watched on the Magnolia Network the day of or the next day streaming on Discovery+ and HBO Max.

However, the real question we should be asking is, will the new project have shiplap?

Why The Boeing 747-400 Was A Turning Point For Two-Pilot Cockpits

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Vytautas Kielaitis | Shutterstock | Simple Flying

For almost two decades, operating the Boeing 747 meant having three people on the flight deck. Alongside the captain and first officer, a dedicated flight engineer monitored the jumbo jet's complex systems, managed fuel distribution, checked engine parameters, and ensured its electrical, hydraulic, and pneumatic systems operated correctly. But the introduction of the 747-400 changed this longstanding arrangement, demonstrating that even one of the world's largest commercial aircraft could be operated by just two pilots.

However, the 747-400 was not the first commercial aircraft to adopt a two-pilot cockpit. Boeing 757 and 767 had already demonstrated the advantages of computerized flight decks, while other manufacturers had experimented with similar arrangements on four-engine aircraft. Nevertheless, the 747-400 represented a major milestone in bringing two-pilot operations to the jumbo-jet category. How did Boeing eliminate the flight engineer, what happened to the responsibilities previously assigned to that position, and how did the change influence subsequent generations of commercial aircraft?

When the original Boeing 747 entered commercial service with Pan American World Airways on January 22, 1970, its flight deck reflected late-1960s technology. The aircraft was considerably more complex than previous commercial jetliners, featuring four engines, extensive fuel systems, multiple hydraulic circuits, and sophisticated environmental controls. As a result, Boeing designed the original 747-100 to operate with three flight deck crew members: a captain, a first officer, and a flight engineer. This arrangement continued with the subsequent 747-200 and 747-300 generations. Boeing's official 747 historical overview identifies these three aircraft as the original Classics generation.

On most aircraft, the flight engineer sat in a dedicated seat behind the two pilots, facing a large instrument panel with hundreds of switches, gauges, indicators, and circuit breakers. While the captain and first officer concentrated on flying, navigation, and communication, the flight engineer monitored the aircraft's technical condition and performed numerous operational tasks. This included checking engine performance, managing fuel transfers between tanks, monitoring electrical generators, maintaining hydraulic pressure, and controlling cabin pressurization. On a four-engine aircraft as large as the 747, these responsibilities created a considerable workload, especially during departure, cruise, and abnormal operating conditions. The Boeing 747 flight deck historical overview compares the original cockpit arrangement with its modern successor.

Importantly, the flight engineer was not an additional pilot. The position required specialist technical training and a detailed understanding of aircraft systems, allowing the two pilots to concentrate on their primary flying responsibilities. The arrangement was also common across earlier generations of four-engine commercial aircraft, including the Boeing 707 and Douglas DC-8. However, as electronic systems became more sophisticated, aircraft manufacturers began to question whether a dedicated third crew member was still necessary. Boeing's subsequent 757 and 767 programs would answer that question.

The transition toward two-pilot commercial aircraft was already underway when Boeing began developing its 757 and 767 programs. Both aircraft entered service in the early 1980s, introducing advanced digital avionics and electronic flight displays that significantly reduced the need for conventional cockpit instrumentation. The 757 entered commercial service with Eastern Air Lines in January 1983, while the 767 followed later that year. Their integrated systems demonstrated that many functions previously requiring dedicated monitoring could be handled through computerized aircraft equipment. NASA's historical research into cockpit automation documents the technological changes introduced with the two aircraft, showing that the development was not entirely straightforward.

Boeing initially faced regulatory and industry resistance to operating larger aircraft with only two pilots. The 1981 Presidential Task Force on Aircraft Crew Complement examined whether advanced automation could safely absorb the responsibilities traditionally assigned to flight engineers. Its findings supported two-pilot operations and helped establish the regulatory foundation for aircraft such as the 767. NASA's publication, Human Factors of Advanced Technology Aircraft, explains how Engine Indication and Crew Alerting System (EICAS) technology became central to this transition. The system was even described as an electronic flight engineer because it could monitor aircraft systems and alert pilots when intervention was necessary.

Contrary to popular belief, the Boeing 747-400 was not the first four-engine jet aircraft certified for two-pilot operation. That distinction belongs to the Lockheed JetStar, the first quadjet designed for two-pilot operation, introduced into service in 1961. Meanwhile, the British Aerospace BAe 146, which first flew in 1981 and entered commercial service in 1983, is recognized as the first commercial passenger quadjet certified for a two-pilot flight deck. Before these developments, four-engine mainline jetliners traditionally required a flight engineer. Thus, the 747-400 did not invent the concept, but it extended that philosophy to the jumbo-jet market.

First quadjet overall with two-pilot operations

First commercial passenger quadjet certified for two-pilot operations

Established integrated systems monitoring

Extended two-pilot philosophy to the jumbo jet

Boeing already had the technological foundations in place when it decided to modernize its flagship aircraft. However, transferring the concept to the 747 required a much more extensive redesign than simply removing the flight engineer's seat.

As the last remaining commercial 747 aircraft near retirement, it still represents one of the most iconic eras in American aviation.

The Boeing 747-400 introduced a completely redesigned flight deck that replaced the traditional analog instrumentation with a computerized glass cockpit. Instead of presenting aircraft information through hundreds of individual gauges and switches, the new arrangement consolidated essential data into six cathode-ray tube (CRT) displays. Two primary flight displays and two navigation displays provided the pilots with essential flight information, while the central displays presented engine parameters and aircraft systems information. According to Airport Technology's technical overview of the 747-400, these displays measured about eight inches by eight inches and incorporated flight control, navigation, engine indication, and crew alerting information.

One of the most striking changes involved the number of instruments and controls in the cockpit. The integrated digital avionics systems reduced the number of flight deck lights, gauges, and switches from approximately 971 on the 747-300 to just 365 on the 747-400. This represented a reduction of more than 60%, illustrating how much information had been consolidated into electronic displays. The Aviamagazine Boeing 747 factsheet documents this transformation.

The aircraft's development also benefited from technology introduced on the 757 and 767. Boeing incorporated integrated avionics, computerized flight management, and more sophisticated monitoring systems, allowing information previously distributed across the flight engineer's panel to be presented directly to the pilots. The 747-400 entered commercial service with Northwest Airlines on February 9, 1989, marking a significant departure from the original jumbo jet's cockpit philosophy. Boeing-747.com's flight deck history explains how the redesigned cockpit absorbed the functions of the traditional engineering station.

As a result, the working environment for pilots changed substantially. However, the most important question was not how many instruments Boeing removed, but how the aircraft continued performing the responsibilities previously assigned to the third crew member.

Boeing did not transfer every flight engineer responsibility to the captain and first officer. Instead, it redesigned the aircraft's systems so that computers could continuously monitor many operating parameters and present the information requiring attention to the remaining crew members. One of the most important technologies was the Engine Indication and Crew Alerting System (EICAS), which consolidated engine information and aircraft system alerts into electronic displays. EICAS could identify abnormal conditions and communicate relevant information to both pilots, thus replacing a flight engineer who monitored hundreds of individual instruments.

The flight engineer's traditional responsibilities extended well beyond engine monitoring, and the new automated system had to cover them. The 747-400's integrated systems provided information about fuel distribution, electrical power, hydraulic systems, environmental controls, and other essential aircraft functions. Pilots could access additional system information through dedicated display pages, while alerts helped prioritize conditions requiring their attention. The Boeing 747-400 technical overview describes the integrated avionics architecture, including systems supplied by Rockwell Collins and Honeywell. In other words, the functions once spread across numerous instruments were consolidated into fewer electronic displays.

Nevertheless, automation did not eliminate the need for pilots to understand the aircraft's technical systems. The captain and first officer remained responsible for monitoring the aircraft, interpreting warnings, and deciding how to respond to abnormal situations. The computers could identify problems and provide information, but they could not replace the judgment required when making operational decisions. This distinction was important because the introduction of advanced avionics also created new human-factor challenges, including the possibility of pilots becoming overly dependent on automation. NASA's research into the introduction of new cockpit technology examined precisely these issues, including pilot workload, training, and the interaction between flight crews and computerized systems.

In short, the 747-400 redistributed responsibilities rather than eliminating an entire operational function. Integrated aircraft systems began performing the flight engineer's traditional monitoring tasks, while the remaining crew members supervised them. This also changed the economics of operating the jumbo jet, particularly on long-haul routes where crew scheduling and staffing represented significant recurring expenses.

Technological advancements in the cockpit enable modern aircraft to fly with only two pilots.

Many airlines had important commercial implications with the introduction of the two-pilot 747-400 configuration. Removing the dedicated flight engineer position meant operators no longer needed to roster a third specialist for every flight, reducing cockpit staffing requirements and simplifying crew scheduling. On long-haul operations, where extended duty periods could require additional pilots, this was particularly valuable. It also made the 747-400 more consistent with the two-pilot philosophy already established on the Boeing 757 and 767, helping airlines operating multiple Boeing aircraft families benefit from a more standardized approach to cockpit operations.

The 747-400's commercial success demonstrated the importance of this modernization. Boeing produced 694 examples of the variant, making it the most commercially successful passenger generation of the jumbo jet family. Its appeal was not based exclusively on the new cockpit, as the aircraft also introduced winglets, improved engines, greater range, and other aerodynamic and structural refinements. Nevertheless, the two-pilot flight deck was among its most consequential operational improvements. The aircraft's success helped establish the expectation that even large four-engine widebodies could operate without a dedicated flight engineer.

Boeing continued this philosophy with the 747-8, the latest production generation of the jumbo jet. Introduced in passenger and freighter configurations, the 747-8 retained the two-pilot flight deck while incorporating further advances in avionics and electronic displays. Its cockpit included technology derived from the Boeing 787 program, although Boeing maintained sufficient commonality with the 747-400 to support a shared type rating following appropriate differences training. The aircraft therefore continued the transition that had begun with the -400, rather than returning to the three-person flight deck of earlier generations.

The 747-8 ultimately demonstrated that the two-pilot arrangement was not a temporary solution specific to the 747-400. It had become the established operating philosophy for the entire modern jumbo-jet family. However, the broader question remains relevant as commercial aircraft become increasingly automated: how much responsibility should be delegated to computers, and how can manufacturers ensure that pilots remain fully capable of managing the aircraft when those systems encounter problems?

The Boeing 747-400 demonstrated that technological advances could fundamentally change how airlines operated their largest aircraft, replacing extensive manual monitoring with integrated computer systems and electronic displays. The transition also helped establish a new relationship between pilots and automation, with computers handling routine monitoring tasks while flight crews retained responsibility for aircraft control and operational decisions.

The contrast between the 747-300 and 747-400 remains particularly striking. The former required three flight deck crew members and analog cockpit lights, gauges, and switches, while the latter operated with two pilots and a glass cockpit. The subsequent 747-8 continued the two-pilot configuration, confirming that Boeing considered the arrangement integral to the jumbo jet's modern design philosophy.

Although the older Lockheed JetStar and BAe 146 had already shown that four-engine aircraft could operate with two pilots, the 747-400 took this concept to an entirely different level in commercial aviation. It proved that aircraft size and engine count did not automatically determine cockpit crew requirements. The 747’s greatest legacy was demonstrating that computerization could transform even the world's most recognizable jumbo jet.

Chocolate-Covered Caramel Apple Slices Are the Easiest Treats for Fall Get-Togethers

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

These chocolate-covered caramel apple slices turn the fruit into a glorified candy bar. Bring them to Friendsgiving or keep them in the fridge for a quick weekday snack.

With two little girls who have been loving all things fall these past couple months, my social algorithm has been filled with cozy crafts, Halloween costume ideas, and, of course, all the seasonal treats. When I scrolled to these chocolate-covered apple slices on a stick from @Crowded kitchen, I knew my daughters would go crazy for them.

Whole, uncut candy-coated apples are too big and too difficult for them to eat, but their eyes widen with delight every time we see them in the store. That’s exactly why this tip is genius. All the chocolate, caramel and fun toppings of a whole apple, but served in easy-to-enjoy slices. My girls loved decorating these treats as much as they loved eating them.

And to be honest, even though they’re easy for kids, they’re easier for adults just the same! Plus, the slices are shareable. This format makes them ideal if you’re looking for simple Friendsgiving ideas or low-lift Thanksgiving desserts.

You’ll start by cutting 1/2-inch thick slices from a whole apple. I like to trim the curviest part first and leave it for a snack, while focusing on the broader part of the apple for the slices. You will also need to strategically work around the core and pick out any seeds before proceeding. From there, you can either insert a candy stick or simply use a bamboo skewer, as I did here.

For the chocolate coating, I prefer to use almond bark chocolate coating instead of traditional chocolate bars or chips. It melts more smoothly and sets quickly without the fussy tempering required for real chocolate.

Cut the apples lengthwise into 1/2-inch thick slices. Insert a stick or skewer into one end of each slice. Cover a baking sheet in parchment, and set aside.

In a microwave-safe bowl, heat the almond bark in 30-second intervals until melted, stirring after each interval.

Spoon the melted chocolate coating over the apple slices, and place them on the prepared baking sheet.

Decorate each chocolate apple slice as desired. Allow the chocolate coating to firm up and set at room temperature before enjoying.

I love serving these candy caramel apple slices on a stick because it makes them so easy to eat. My girls loved them because it’s like having giant, fancy lollipops. You can use either a candy stick (you might be able to find them in the baking section of grocery stores) or a bamboo skewer. Candy sticks might not be as readily available, but they are sturdier. Bamboo skewers are effortless to insert and easy to find, but they are a little flimsier, and you have to be careful not to poke yourself while eating the apple.

What candy combo would you make? Let us know in the comments below!

On Friday, Bitcoin briefly plunged below $75,000 as a sharp sell-off in US technology stocks spilled into digital assets.

This came as selling pressure now dominates spot trading on Binance and Coinbase, threatening to push Bitcoin toward $70,000 if a critical support level fails.

CryptoQuant analyst Darkfost reported that Bitcoin investors realized between $657 million and $703 million in losses daily over the past three days, bringing the combined amount to more than $2 billion.

Despite the scale of those losses, weekly net realized profit and loss stood at negative $191 million, reflecting gains realized by other holders during the period.

The losses accelerated after Bitcoin fell below $80,000 and are approaching levels last recorded during the market downturn in early August.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Meanwhile, Darkfost said the 30-day moving average of Bitcoin's spot net volume delta, which measures the difference between aggressive buying and selling, has turned negative on Binance and Coinbase.

At the end of August, the indicator stood at positive $64.8 million on Binance and $64.3 million on Coinbase, reflecting strong demand as Bitcoin rallied from $63,000 toward $85,000 in September.

By Nov. 5, those readings had reversed to negative $28 million and $9.4 million, respectively.

The deterioration began on Coinbase in October, while Binance initially stayed positive. Both exchanges are now recording more aggressive selling than buying, showing how the weakness has spread across major trading venues.

The reversal comes as Bitcoin struggles to recover from Thursday's market rout, which sent the S&P 500 down 4.36% and the Nasdaq Composite down 5.52%.

Concerns about artificial intelligence valuations and the prospect of tighter restrictions on data-center development following the Democratic sweep in the US midterm elections drove a sharp decline in technology stocks.

The rout erased much of Wednesday's post-election relief rally and exposed Bitcoin's vulnerability to broader risk aversion.

However, Bitcoin's problems extend beyond the equity-market downturn.

CryptoQuant head of research Julio Moreno has identified declining Bitcoin demand and contracting stablecoin liquidity as two major challenges facing the market.

Without stronger spot buying or renewed capital inflows, further selling could encounter insufficient demand to stabilize prices.

Bitcoin's fragile market position faces another test after the US economy added 165,000 jobs in October, more than double economists' expectations of 65,000.

The unemployment rate fell to 4.1% from 4.2% in September, while average hourly earnings increased 0.4%. Hiring figures for August and September were also revised higher by a combined 84,000 jobs, reinforcing evidence of a resilient labor market.

The stronger-than-expected report increased expectations of another Federal Reserve interest rate hike in December. According to Reuters, futures traders raised the probability of a quarter-point increase to approximately 80%, from 75% before the release.

US Treasury yields rose in response, with the benchmark 10-year yield reaching approximately 4.88% in early trading. Higher yields could further weaken demand for Bitcoin by making interest-bearing assets more attractive and reducing investors' appetite for risk.

However, much of the anticipated December tightening was already priced into financial markets before Friday's report.

Fabian Dori, chief investment officer at Swiss digital asset bank Sygnum, told CryptoSlate that investors should watch longer-term Treasury yields, which could determine whether financial conditions tighten further.

For Bitcoin, the immediate concern is that additional macroeconomic pressure could accelerate selling in a market already struggling with declining demand and contracting stablecoin liquidity.

Bitget analyst Lewis Huang identified $76,500 as a near-term support level, warning that a sustained break below it, particularly alongside rising Treasury yields and a stronger dollar, could deepen the correction.

CryptoQuant analyst Darkfost placed the more consequential threshold at $75,000. A failure to defend that level could send Bitcoin toward $70,000, where a significant concentration of investors previously acquired their holdings.

That concentration could provide support if buyers emerge around their cost basis, although continued selling could overwhelm demand at those levels.

The next indication of whether Bitcoin can stabilize will come from spot trading activity and stablecoin liquidity. Without a recovery in either, the market risks testing the $70,000 region even if the Federal Reserve's December decision delivers no further surprise.

Iran Is Handing Out Iodine Pills Near Pickaxe Mountain. Here’s What a Nuclear Strike There Would Actually Do.

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Whether or not the U.S. ever drops a nuke on the granite complex, the physics of attacking something buried 100 meters underground explains why Tehran is telling people to “get inside, stay inside.”

Since the U.S. and Israel launched a military campaign against Iran on February 28, Washington and Tehran have sparred over whether Pickaxe Mountain (Kūh-e Kolang Gaz Lā) in the Karkas Mountains in central Iran is the secretive site of the country’s nuclear enrichment efforts. In past statements, U.S. President Donald Trump has made vague threats regarding the site, saying that the U.S. would “take care of it.” In September, he added: “We notice there’s a little activity at Pickaxe. I would advise Iran not to get cute because we will have to hit them very hard.”

What that means is anyone’s guess, but this week political commentator Tucker Carlson wrote on X—without offering evidence—that the U.S. was possibly “within days” of launching a nuclear strike on the site. The White House responded that Carlson has “no insight into military planning,” and Trump himself ruled out using nuclear weapons against Iran in mid-October. Still, Trump told reporters Thursday that a decision on renewed strikes is coming “soon,” and the Iranians, at least, are behaving as though they take the nuclear scenario seriously.

That’s the part worth understanding. According to The Times, Iran’s health ministry is distributing iodine tablets to residents within a two-to-three-kilometer radius of nuclear facilities, including Pickaxe. A video circulating on Iranian social media walks viewers through a hypothetical nuclear explosion at the site, listing distances to nearby cities—17 miles to Natanz, 25 to Kashan, 117 to Isfahan, 223 to Tehran—and offering three instructions: “Get inside, stay inside and listen for updates.” It warns that people near the site might have only 10 to 15 minutes to do so. One Iranian analyst told the paper, “If America does not use a nuclear weapon against Pickaxe Mountain, it will be a miracle.”

So what, exactly, are they preparing for?

Potassium iodide doesn’t protect you from radiation in general. It does one narrow job: it saturates the thyroid with stable iodine so the gland can’t absorb radioactive iodine-131, a fission product that concentrates in the thyroid and is a leading cause of post-fallout thyroid cancer, especially in children. I-131 has a half-life of about eight days, which is why the pills are a first-week countermeasure rather than a long-term one. Handing them out is what a government does when its planning assumption is a fission event upwind of populated areas—as opposed to a conventional bombing, which produces no I-131 at all.

Pickaxe first entered the public record in September 2020, two months after an explosion destroyed an above-ground centrifuge-assembly workshop at Natanz. Iran’s then-nuclear chief, Ali Akbar Salehi, said work had begun on a “more modern, larger and more comprehensive” replacement inside a nearby mountain. Satellite imagery since then shows four fortified tunnel portals, internal roads, possible ventilation shafts, and an expanding security perimeter—but International Atomic Energy Agency inspectors have never been inside, and no schematics exist in the public domain. Analysts say the halls could house centrifuge production, an enrichment plant, or protected storage for Iran’s roughly 440 kilograms of 60-percent-enriched uranium. Back in July, The Wall Street Journal reported that Israeli intelligence told the U.S. that Iran had moved centrifuges to the site following the 12-day war that started in June 2025.

Estimates put parts of the complex 80 to 100 meters (roughly 260 to 330 feet) underground, in granite. For comparison, the GBU-57 Massive Ordnance Penetrator—the 30,000-pound bunker buster used for the first time in combat during Operation Midnight Hammer in June 2025—is generally credited with penetrating around 200 feet of earth, and considerably less through hard rock. At Fordow, the U.S. compensated by dropping six MOPs in sequence down each of two ventilation shafts, each bomb boring deeper than the last. It’s not publicly known whether Pickaxe has comparable shafts to exploit, or how many MOPs remain in the inventory.

“Pickaxe Mountain is a deeply buried granite fortification, and it will be difficult to destroy the facility even with the heaviest nonnuclear U.S. munitions, such as the GBU-57 Massive Ordnance Penetrator,” the Center for Strategic & International Studies reported in September 2026. “While military strikes may degrade or temporarily disable Pickaxe Mountain, continuously bombing Iranian nuclear infrastructure is unsustainable.”

The GBU-43/B Massive Ordnance Air Blast bomb, or “Mother of All Bombs,” which CBS News reported is also in Pentagon planning, is a different animal entirely: it works by creating overpressure, not by blasting through rock. It could kill personnel in tunnels open to the air and wreck surface infrastructure, but it’s not a bunker buster.

What a nuclear strike would do—and why that’s the dirty option

The U.S. arsenal holds both strategic nuclear weapons (the ones capable of leveling entire cities) and tactical nuclear weapons, such as the lower-yield B61 family of gravity bombs. Newsweek reports one scenario in which the U.S. would first soften the site with several GBU-57s before following up with a B61. The weapon most often mentioned is the B61-11, the Pentagon’s only dedicated earth-penetrating nuclear bomb, with a reported yield of up to around 400 kilotons—roughly 25 times Hiroshima. The newest variant, the B61-13, pairs the B61-12’s precision-guided tail kit with a yield of up to about 360 kilotons. It’s carried only by strategic bombers like the B-2, entered production in 2025, and was pitched by the Pentagon as an option against “harder and large-area military targets.”

Here’s the catch: None of these bombs goes deep. The B61-11 is designed to punch only a few meters into the ground before detonating, the B61-12 can manage a few meters at most, and the B61-13 would go off at or near the surface, relying on sheer yield. A weapon that goes off even a short distance underground transfers far more of its energy into the rock as a shock wave than an air burst does, which is how it can collapse tunnels a hundred meters down. But a few meters is nowhere near deep enough to contain the explosion. Underground test data suggests that fully containing a blast in the 400-kiloton range would require burying it something like 800 meters deep. No deliverable bomb comes close to that.

So whichever bomb is used, the burst behaves, for fallout purposes, like the worst kind of ground burst. The fireball vaporizes thousands of tons of granite and soil, mixes it with the bomb’s own fission products—iodine-131, cesium-137, strontium-90—and lofts the whole radioactive mess into a plume that drops back to Earth downwind over hours and days. That’s the fallout Iran’s iodine pills are for. The centrifuges and enriched uranium inside would be pulverized and dispersed too, though uranium itself is a comparatively minor radiological hazard; the bomb’s fission debris does the real damage.

Where it lands depends on the weather. Prevailing winds over central Iran run broadly west to east, which would push a plume away from Tehran and toward Yazd, the eastern provinces, and potentially Afghanistan and Pakistan. The heaviest fallout falls within tens of miles, so India Today’s questions about whether detectable radiation could reach South Asia aren’t crazy, though doses that far out would be low. Those “10 to 15 minutes” in the Iranian video are about the lethal early fallout close in, which is why the advice is to get indoors immediately rather than to flee.

If a strike happens over the weekend, confirmation won’t depend on anyone’s press release. Seismometers around the world would register the event within minutes, and a nuclear explosion looks different from an earthquake or a string of conventional bombs: it causes a sharp, compressional first motion rather than the shearing signature of a fault slipping. The Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty Organization operates a global network of seismic, infrasound, and radionuclide stations that routinely catches North Korean tests; its air samplers would pick up radioactive xenon isotopes—which nothing but fission produces—within a day or two. A MOP strike, by contrast, would show up as small, repeated surface-coupled thumps with no xenon.

For all the attention Carlson’s post has drawn, most experts expect a nuclear option to remain a remote possibility.

“I don’t think it’s very likely—as rash as President Trump is, I think he knows what would be the great implications of that,” Yale University’s Arash Azizi, a Middle East analyst, told DW News. “I should say though that I’ve heard the same from Iranian officials in the last few months. It is a worry that they have that he will cross that line that has of course never been crossed since 1945.”

Other experts speaking with Newsweek were blunter, calling such an attack “catastrophically stupid”—not primarily because of the fallout, but because of what it would signal. The first use of a nuclear weapon since 1945, against a non-nuclear state, would, in their assessment, undercut the Non-Proliferation Treaty and give other governments a vivid reason to conclude that only a deterrent of their own keeps them safe.

The realistic range of outcomes, then, runs from a negotiated pause—Qatari mediators were still working the phones Thursday—to a conventional strike package built around MOPs, cruise missiles against the portals and ventilation shafts, and possibly a MOAB. Even that leaves open the question CSIS raised: a facility 100 meters down in granite can be sealed, cut off, and set back, but “destroyed” is a harder word to earn. Fordow was declared “obliterated” in June 2025, and the argument over what that meant hasn’t ended. Pickaxe is deeper underground, so the likelihood of it surviving a nonnuclear strike is higher.

For now, a nuclear strike remains only a hypothetical scenario. But Iranian officials are reportedly handing out iodine pills and telling people how far they live from a mountain. That tells you what they’re planning for—and the physics of bunker-busting explains why.

Coventry boss Lampard blames trio of VAR errors for Everton loss

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Syndicated reporting provided by Stats Perform and reviewed by the SoccerNews editorial team.

Frank Lampard felt VAR "didn't do its job" during Coventry City's 3-1 defeat to Everton, arguing three huge decisions went against his side.

Two goals from Thierno Barry and one from Kiernan Dewsbury-Hall flipped Friday's game on its head, after Yann Gboho put Coventry in front early in the second half.

But things could have been very different, had VAR officials Timothy Wood and Gary Beswick seen three big incidents differently.

Lampard believed Coventry should have had a penalty in either half, first when James Tarkowski appeared to pull Caleb Yirenkyi's hair and then when Taiwo Awoniyi was clipped from behind by Vitalii Mykolenko.

Additionally, Barry's second goal was checked for a potential handball but swiftly cleared, after the ball skipped up off the turf, hit the Frenchman, and squirmed past Carl Rushworth.

"The first-half decision on Tarkowski got checked, and seeing it back, he pulls on Caleb's hair. I thought the rules were clear," Lampard told Sky Sports.

"I know they've changed the rules so it's not a red card, I get that, but he’s pulling on his hair and [Yirenkyi] can't get his momentum to jump. I think it's a foul and a penalty and they decided no.

"I thought the one on Taiwo running through – it's a clear foul that the referee didn't give it. I can't believe that, because it was clear. Maybe he waited for the VAR because it was on the borderline.

"But as I see it, Taiwo's foot is entering the box and his knee and the back of his knee are on the line of the box, which is a penalty. That goes against us with VAR.

"And then Barry's second goal comes off his hand. I hate to sound like a moaner. I've worked here and know how momentum can swing the games.

"When big decisions change games, it's disappointing. VAR is there for that, and it didn't do its job."

Frank Lampard gives his verdict on tonight's defeat.  https://t.co/QBoH3jzUHw

— Coventry City (@CoventryCityFC) November 6, 2026

Coventry have lost seven of their first 10 league games to start a season for just the third time in their history, also doing so in 1919-20 (nine defeats) and 1920-21 (seven), which were their first two campaigns in the Football League.

And, on the last six occasions where a former Everton manager has faced the Toffees in the Premier League, they have lost all six matches – they were Marco Silva (three times), Sean Dyche (twice) and Lampard on Friday.

The Sky Blues sit 16th in the Premier League ahead of the international break, though they could drop into the relegation zone if results go against them this weekend.

Sony’s New Hello Kitty Headphones Come With an Ultra-Chic Carrying Case That Deserves Its Own Spotlight

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Available now for preorder, the limited-edition collection features three wireless, noise-reduction headphones, each paired with a carrying case and tote bag, with prices starting at $104.99.

All products and services featured are independently chosen by editors. However, Billboard may receive a commission on orders placed through its retail links, and the retailer may receive certain auditable data for accounting purposes.

Attention Hello Kitty fans: Your next favorite fashion accessory is designed to carry your headphones.

Sony has teamed up with the iconic Sanrio character for a limited-edition collection featuring three models from Sony’s existing headphone lineup, including the WH-CH530, WH-CH730N and WH-1000XM6. Each pair of headphones offers different noise-reduction technology and comes in either black or pink. While the headphones themselves have a simple design, the accompanying accessories are where the collection really stands out.

Each headphone comes with a black tote bag and a grunge-inspired black Hello Kitty carrying case that can clip onto a bag or clothing. The case features Hello Kitty’s signature ears and bow, along with headphone-shaped storage compartments that make it look like she’s wearing a pair of headphones herself. Available now for preorder with a scheduled release before the holidays, the collection makes a fun Christmas gift for music lovers and Hello Kitty fans alike. The WH-CH530 features noise reduction rather than full noise cancellation.

The most affordable option in the collection, the WH-CH530 is a lightweight pair of on-ear headphones. They offer up to 55 hours of battery life, so you can go several days without reaching for the charger. Need a quick boost? A three-minute charge provides up to 1.5 hours of playback. They also feature a built-in microphone for calls.

If you want to tune out background noise, the WH-CH730N offers noise cancellation to help make listening more focused. Its Ambient Sound Mode also lets outside sounds come through when you need to hear what’s happening around you. The foldable design makes these headphones easy to pack for trips, while the battery lasts up to 50 hours with noise cancellation on or 75 hours with it off. They also include a microphone designed to make phone calls clearer.

As of publication, the WH-1000XM6 is sold out in pink, but still available in black.

For those looking for Sony’s most advanced option in this collection, the WH-1000XM6 offers top-tier noise cancellation and high-quality sound. Its microphones and processors constantly adjust to help block out surrounding noise while keeping your music clear. You can also switch between noise cancellation and ambient sound when you want to stay aware of your surroundings. The headphones offer up to 30 hours of battery life and a quick three-minute charge provides up to three hours of playback.

Zelda Producer Eiji Aonuma Talks About The Benefits Of "Long-Term-Tenured Staff" At Nintendo

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

"It contributes to some of the sense of continuity"

One morsel from Nintendo's latest Ask the Developer interview revealed how the team behind the Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake got the artist "who had created illustrations for the N64 version" to draw a new map for the Switch 2 release.

It's not every day developers reviving older games can directly consult original team members, but this is just one of many examples at Nintendo. Zelda producer, Eiji Aonuma, has now elaborated on the benefits of Nintendo's "long-term tenured staff members", explaining how it provides "balance" and the "sense of continuity" when crafting projects like the remake.

Here's what he had to say during a chat with GameSpot:

Eiji Aonuma: "I think you see a real benefit to having some very long-term-tenured staff members around who have shared the same experiences over time because every time you get to the next step, it’s easier to discuss how to proceed, or if you’re going to do something new. When you have new staff coming in, of course you want to make sure that you’re bringing everyone up with a lot of discussion, explaining how to do things, why you’re doing things certain ways, as well as the language that you’re using, but that takes some more time as well. So you need a bit of a balance. You don’t want your new staff doing things by rote or without understanding the reasons, you have to invest there. And so you do see the Zelda team change over time, but there is a continuity of core staff, certainly at least from one project to the next, so that we have that base that we can build from and bring up new staff within it. And this is something that I think Nintendo has done for a very long time, even since Miyamoto originally worked on projects like this. And it contributes to some of the sense of continuity that you do feel in games in the Zelda franchise, for example."

As the source notes, Nintendo's "unique culture" of retaining employees "for years" is often quite the contrast to local companies and studios that arguably don't show long-serving employees enough respect, and may not always consider their extensive experience and history as a positive influence moving forward.

In some cases, the dismissal of veteran staff at certain game companies has resulted in younger and less experienced teams navigating the development of remakes, remasters and sequels without any assistance or advice, as there are no long-term staff members left at the company to consult (or direct contact lines). So it's great that Nintendo seems to have found the right balance.

Bayern Munich: the length of absence is known for Ismael Saibari

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

This is a huge blow for Bayern Munich. Out injured against Borussia Dortmund (2-2) after a clash with the opposing goalkeeper, Ismael Saibari will have to be patient. According to information from BILDthe Moroccan international (36 caps) will be away from the field for at least three months. A long unavailability for the attacking midfielder, whose knee and ankle had suffered a worrying twist during the match. The Moroccan midfielder suffers from a ruptured syndesmotic ligament in his right ankle and has already undergone successful surgery.

This prolonged absence represents very bad news for Bayern, already facing several physical problems in its squad. Serge Gnabry and Michael Olise were also forced to leave the pitch against Borussia Dortmund. While Vincent Kompany continues his convalescence, the Bavarian club will have to cope without Saibari for several months, with the hope of recovering their Moroccan international as quickly as possible.

Michael Sanft, Attorney Who Represented Keffe D in 2Pac Murder Trial, Dies at 54

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Sanft put together a team and took on Davis' case pro bono.

Michael Sanft, the defense attorney who represented Duane “Keffe D” Davis during the 2Pac murder trial, has died, FOX5 Las Vegas reported. He was 54 years old.

Sanft put together a team and took on Davis’ case pro bono while serving as the lead trial attorney for the Vegas-based SOS Injury Lawyers, where he has tried more than 150 cases. After the highly publicized trial, he told FOX5 Las Vegas that he didn’t have any regrets. Related Kenny MacPherson, Veteran Music Publisher and Big Deal Music Co-Founder, Dies at 71 Diddy Moved From New Jersey Prison to Similar Facility in Pennsylvania Weezer to Receive Legend of Live Award at 2026 Billboard Live Music Summit Explore 2Pac Tupac Shakur See latest videos, charts and news

“Maybe if I had a psychic sitting next to me to help discern who should be on the jury,” he told the outlet. “We had the best team possible in this case, from the motion practice work on this appeal to our investigator to our firm here at SOS Injury. We all did our part. I don’t see what I could’ve done differently.”

Thirty years after the 25-year-old Tupac Shakur was killed in a drive-by shooting in Las Vegas following a Mike Tyson fight, the now-63-year-old Keffe D was found guilty for his role in orchestrating the killing and sentenced to 40 years in prison. “You don’t get to take a man from his family, you don’t get to take his very life, his whole future from him and just walk away from it. Especially when that man is Tupac Shakur,” Judge Carli Kierny told Davis as she handed down his sentencing. “It took us 30 years, but at this time you are going to face justice for what you did on Sept. 7, 1996.”

Sanft reportedly died on Thursday night in Las Vegas. No cause of death has been confirmed as the details surrounding his passing have been limited so far.

How Does Michael Jackson’s Son Prince Feel About Jaafar Playing The Late Singer?

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Discover the latest entertainment updates and trailers.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

Harry Potter’s Snape Actor Paapa Essiedu Shares A Refreshing Take On The Backlash He’s Faced

Sunrise On The Reaping First Reactions Are Here. As A Book Reader I'm So Hyped One Performance Is The Standout

I Think One Wild Horse Nine Performance Is 100% Oscar-Worthy (And I’m Loving That Reviews Agree)

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The huge success of Michael took me by surprise. I knew it would be popular, as I’m a child of the ‘80s and I remember just how huge Michael Jackson was. In fact, the first cassette tape I ever owned was Thriller, which I got for Christmas in 1982 or 1983. Still, I didn’t think Michael would crush the 2026 movie schedule the way it did when it rang up north of a billion dollars at the box office. As for my opinion on the movie, I think it’s…well…fine.

However, Jaafar Jackson’s performance is spectacular. I honestly think he should be nominated for an Oscar, though I’m skeptical that he will be. Obviously, I’m not alone in my praise for Michael’s nephew’s acting, singing, and dancing skills shown off in the film. His performance has been lauded by virtually everyone, even those who didn't like Michael. Another person who loved the performance is Prince Jackson, Michael’s youngest son and Jaafar’s cousin.

Prince recently spoke to The Associated Press and talked about his cousin playing his dad, and he definitely loved it. Prince said:

I’m so thankful that the world has embraced his performance and his embodiment of my father in the way in which they did. But I think that it would be incredible for him to get the acknowledgement from his peers in this field of the creative artistry, the determination and the discipline I think that went into this.

The performance by Jaafar wasn’t luck, either. He didn’t rely on his family name (Jaafar’s father is the late Jermaine Jackson) to coast through the movie. He worked his tail off to give that performance. His practice sessions have become the stuff of legend already. Prince noted this in his comments as well, saying:

He’s a little bit older than me. We grew up together, but I watched my cousin work his butt off. He locked himself in our family home in Hayvenhurst for weeks at a time. And we would see each other regularly, weekends or whatever. But, during that time when he was working, didn’t see Jaafar. [The] only time we saw him was when he was dancing or sweating or locked in his room when he was doing his character research.

Unfortunately, the Academy often seems to forget movies that are released in the first half of any year, as Michael was when it came out in April. That means that Jaafar Jackson is likely going to be overlooked come Oscar nomination morning in January. It’s also a big, splashy movie about a man, Michael, who isn’t without serious controversy. Controversy, it’s worth noting, that the movie doesn’t get into.

I don’t think that’s on Jaafar, though, and for his performance, he is worthy of great praise. It’s nice to see his cousin (and the son of the man he’s playing) lavish that upon him. Maybe it’ll bring more attention to the people who vote. In the meantime, if you want to watch the performance again, it’s available with the Starz add-on to a Prime subscription.

Hugh Scott is the Syndication Editor for CinemaBlend. Before CinemaBlend, he was the managing editor for Suggest.com and Gossipcop.com, covering celebrity news and debunking false gossip. He has been in the publishing industry for almost two decades, covering pop culture – movies and TV shows, especially – with a keen interest and love for Gen X culture, the older influences on it, and what it has since inspired. He graduated from Boston University with a degree in Political Science but cured himself of the desire to be a politician almost immediately after graduation.

APOD: 2026 November 7 – De Gerlache-Kocher Massif

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Discover the cosmos! Each day a different image or photograph of our fascinating universe is featured, along with a brief explanation written by a professional astronomer.

Explanation: De Gerlache-Kocher massif looms on the visible horizon in this close-up of the edge of the Moon. The mountainous formation rises about 4 kilometers above its surroundings and is located very close to the lunar south pole. Boasting permanently shadowed crater floors that likely contain deposits of water ice and peaks that receive almost continuous sunlight for solar power generation, the south polar region is consider a prime location for the first human settlement on the Moon. In fact, De Gerlache-Kocher massif itself has been considered as a potential landing site for future crewed Artemis missions to the Moon. The remarkable telescopic image of the massif at the lunar limb was captured on October 31. Then, a favorable libration angle or lunar wobble enhanced this oblique view across the Moon's south pole.Tomorrow's picture: meteoric

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

Trump Has Blamed Everyone for the GOP’s Midterm Meltdown—Except Himself and the Worst Member of His Staff

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Maybe the president shouldn’t have told everyone to pretend he was on the ballot.

(Permanent Musical Accompaniment to the Last Post of the Week from the Blog's Favourite Living Canadian)

Wake up, little Susie. The gravy train went off the tracks. From CNN:

So, the president is a) in a rage because his party got annihilated after he bounced around the country telling everyone to pretend he was on the ballot, which millions of Americans cheerfully did, and b) his solution is to put the least popular primate in his employ in charge of the White House. I'm sure that this bodes well for his relationship with the incoming Congress, considering that any hearings about ICE and draconian immigration policies is bound to have Miller at a witness table.

He, of course, was not "among those" Trump blamed for the loss. He never is.

We have lost some of the highest flying of the flying monkeys in the House of Representatives. Victoria Sparks? Gone. Scott Perry? Gone. Andy Biggs? Gone. Lauren Boebert? Virginia Foxx? Probably gone. However, the Republican reservoir of otherwise unemployable whackadoos remains bottomless. Meet Anthony Constantino, congresscritter-elect from that part of New York we like to call Baja Canada. Here he is, celebrating his victory.

Ladies and gentlemen, your 2028 Republican National Convention keynote speaker.

Ever since the Great Day of Reckoning dawned, I've been watching a number of interviews with the various new faces that have emerged. Specifically, I listened to James Talarico in Texas and Dr. Annie Andrews in South Carolina. and both of them laid out their wish list for congressional reform. Both of them want a ban on congressional stock trading, an improvement supported by an estimated 125 percent of the American population. They both also expressed support for ... wait for it ... term limits.

Whoa, there, rookies. Kept that baby in your pockets, didn't you?

As is well known around the shebeen, term limits are the third worst idea in American politics. (The first two are a balanced budget amendment to the Constitution and the elected judiciary.) They are a poisoned placebo. You end up with legislators led around by the nose by battalions of lobbyists, who end up empowered as the legislature's institutional memory. And that's not to mention the fact that there ought to be no law or policy that tells me I can't vote for somebody for whom I want to vote, assuming they're not dead or incarcerated, and I'm willing to negotiate on the second one.

On the Supreme Court, I'm all in favor of them. Those are appointed positions, not elected ones, and, back in the day, justices regularly returned to private life and their old clients back home. For example, Justice Benjamin Curtis was on the Court for six years, long enough for him to write his classic dissent in Dred Scott v. Sandford. He then quit over money and because he hated Chief Justice Roger Taney. (Good plan.) He went back to Boston and his previous client list.

(In a very odd twist of fate, Curtis returned to Washington as lead counsel for President Andrew Johnson in the latter's impeachment trial before the Senate. He won that one.)

So, no, Senators-elect, stay away from this cheap snake oil. It will make a legislature gag.

XRP Had the Worst Day in Ages, Hyperliquid (HYPE) Awaits Volatility, What Pushes Stellar (XLM) Down? Crypto Market Review

📅 2026-11-07  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

The cryptocurrency market remains under pressure, as most larger altcoins have corrected: large caps lost around 5% on average in the last 24 hours. Key levels have not been broken yet, but the overall trend is still bearish, despite yesterday's recovery.

From a technical standpoint, XRP has moved further into a downtrend, driven by more liquidations of long positions. According to the Nov. 5 snapshot, more than $12 million worth of longs has been liquidated. While the number itself is not critical, the long liquidations suggest that the asset saw an influx of buying, but it was quickly shut down.

The XRP Ledger activated the fixCleanup3_4_0 amendment on November 4 at 14:52 UTC. It removed an exploit that, under certain conditions, let accounts authorized to burn an issuer's tokens also mint them. The native supply of XRP has not been affected, luckily, so illicit actors have not used the exploit.

According to the technical analysis, the price is struggling to hold the important psychological threshold: $1.2 has already been broken, and XRP now trades at approximately $1.2.

The volume influx on the move toward the 21 EMA suggested a possible improvement in market conditions, but low volume on Friday, during the transition into the weekend trading session, disrupted any bullish plans.

As for the future, XRP is clearly moving in a longer-term downtrend; however, we will most likely see a local bounce toward $1.25. An EMA cross between the 100 EMA and the 50 EMA would simply be a signal of further price weakness and trend acceleration (most likely down).

The XRP Ledger has seen substantial growth in various on-chain metrics, which, unfortunately, does not give the price enough support. A lot of the Ledger activity appears to be automated, either trading bots or sophisticated LLM models. The market is mostly driven by speculative interest rather than fundamental network usage.

Hyperliquid fell by almost 9% on Nov. 5 and is now trading below $80, balancing on the 100 EMA, which has been broken temporarily. From a fundamental standpoint, Hyperliquid Strategies (PURR) disclosed that it has applied to become a U.S. futures commission merchant. If approved, it would be able to bring U.S. clients directly to the perpetual markets deployed on Hyperliquid.

In addition to this substantial regulatory milestone, Hyperliquid reported $620 million in quarterly net income, including $803 million in unrealized HYPE gains. Before that, on Oct. 29, Hyperliquid announced a separate ICE-Trade agreement; more details are available on the SEC's official website.

From a technical standpoint, on the daily HYPE/USDT chart, the price has bounced somewhat from a local psychological support level of approximately $77. It has been a recovery point for Hyperliquid, but, unfortunately, it is unlikely to hold if more selling pressure appears.

RSI, as a momentum indicator, is moving toward the 40 zone, so any surge of bearish pressure here could be short-lived if it is mostly speculative. However, with volume rising during the descending local trend, the bearish trend is being reinforced.

Hyperliquid (HYPE) lost its pace by the end of September, and a lower high formed in the first days of October, which is essentially the market's main signal of the end of the rally. Despite the spike at the beginning of November, it is still a lower high, which means the bearish trend is not going anywhere.

This is a relatively hard prediction to make, considering the unpredictable nature of the market. Unfortunately, the price has barely any support left, and the next support is the 200 EMA (black line on the chart), which coincides with the $68 price level.

Volume, on the other hand, is falling, which is either a normal tendency before the weekend trading session or a sign of a weakening trend for now.

Key price levels for HYPE are: $80 as the closest psychological support level, $68 as the 200 EMA, and $89 as the first psychological resistance level. The trendline resistance is at approximately $84, so a breakthrough there should become a foundation for a price recovery.

After Stellar (XLM) fell toward $0.157, there is a good chance that we are seeing accumulation or at least a transition into a sideways trend. The moving averages are not converging, which signals trend acceleration, and in this case it is a bearish one. Whenever moving averages converge, volatility is more likely to spike.

The trend on Stellar is accelerating, while volume remains relatively low and neutral.

It is no coincidence that XLM saw a trend slowdown at approximately $0.15, as it coincides with the point where the previous trend started. The death cross that occurred at the end of October is also a bearish signal.

It was followed by a spike in trading volume, which confirms the significance of the indicator and suggests that the trend may continue.

The latest network milestone for XLM is the release of Bell (Protocol 30), which was set to go live on testnet on November 3. The key change it brought to the network was post-quantum signature verification for smart contracts and millisecond ledger timestamps.

However, the mainnet validator vote, which will make the update available to users on the live network, is set to take place on November 23 at 17:00 UTC.

The bearish price performance on the market was followed by a recorded 500,000 XLM long liquidation. But considering the sheer volume of XLM on exchanges, a sub-1 million liquidation barely affects the price and will most likely have a very minor effect on the market.

From the momentum standpoint, RSI is already at a local bottom, and a further drop is possible, despite RSI already sliding to around 30. An oversold RSI is not really an issue when there is profitable capital present.

Considering the behavior of the market, there is a good chance that most of the buying support was at 1.5-1.6, so a break below the $1.5 support level could trigger a further decline, with a possible acceleration toward $1, though this is unlikely and possible only in a very bearish market.
